L5: Table 1. Application Information
L6: Application type	NDA
Application number(s)	219019
Priority or standard	STANDARD
Submit date(s)	3/28/2024
Received date(s)	3/28/2024
PDUFA goal date	3/29/2025
Division/office	Division of Nonmalignant Hematology (DNH)
Review completion date	Electronic Stamp
Established/proper name	Fitusiran
(Proposed) proprietary name	QFITLIA (fitusiran)
Pharmacologic class	Antithrombin-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA)
Other product name(s)	SAR439774 or ALN-AT3SC
Applicant	GENZYME CORP
Dosage form(s)/formulation(s)	INJECTION, SOLUTION
Dosing regimen	The starting dose of fitusiran is 50 mg once every 2 months. The dose should be adjusted if needed, to maintain antithrombin (AT) activity between 15-35%.
(b) (4)
Applicant-proposed indication(s)/population(s)	Indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older with hemophilia A or B with or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors.
SNOMED CT code for proposed indication disease term(s)1	41788008
Regulatory action	Approval
Approved dosage (if applicable)	The starting dose of fitusiran is 50 mg once subcutaneously every 2 months. The dose should be adjusted, if needed, to maintain AT activity between 15-35%. Monitor AT activity using an FDA-cleared test.
Approved indication(s)/population(s) (if applicable)	Fitusiran is an antithrombin-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients aged 12 years and older with hemophilia A or B with or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors.
SNOMED CT code for approved indication disease term(s)1	41788008

L38: ## I. Executive Summary
L39: ## 1. Overview
L40: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L41: The Applicant, Genzyme Corporation, submitted an NDA for fitusiran (trade name QFITLIA), an antithrombin (AT)-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid, for the following indication: routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients aged 12 years and older with hemophilia A or B with or without factor VIII or IX inhibitors. The proposed starting dosage is 50 mg once subcutaneously (SC) every 2 months. The dose should be adjusted, if needed, to maintain AT activity in the range of 15 to 35%.
L42: The Applicant conducted two adequate and well-controlled trials, in which fitusiran prophylaxis use at 80 mg once monthly (QM) demonstrated a substantial improvement in the annualized bleeding rate (ABR) compared to on-demand treatment in subjects with hemophilia A with inhibitors (HAWI) and hemophilia B with inhibitors (HBWI) (Study EFC14768), and subjects with hemophilia A without inhibitors (HA) and hemophilia B without inhibitors (HB) (Study EFC14769). During development, to mitigate the risk of thrombotic events, an antithrombin-based dose regimen (AT-DR) targeting an AT activity range of 15 to 35% was implemented. Subjects in the single-arm extension study (Study LTE15174), who used the to-be-marketed fitusiran AT-DR, were compared to the on-demand control arms from the randomized controlled parent studies (Study EFC19768 and Study EFC14769). We concluded that this comparison was also an adequate and well-controlled investigation and that it confirmed that the efficacy of fitusiran is preserved with the AT-DR.
L43: Fitusiran has serious risks of thromboembolic events and gallbladder disease (including cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, and the need for cholecystectomy in some patients). These risks and mitigation recommendations will be described in a boxed warning. Hepatotoxicity is also a serious risk that will be described in a Warning and Precaution with recommendations for liver test monitoring. The incidence of these risks appears lower with the use of the AT-DR dose regimen. Contemporaneously with fitusiran's approval, the Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) is clearing a companion diagnostic assay so healthcare providers can accurately assess AT activity and adjust the fitusiran dose and frequency of administration, as needed, to target AT activity of 15 to 35%, which is essential for the safe and effective use of fitusiran.
L44: The application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval, and the signatory authority concurs that the application should be approved. The overall benefit-risk profile is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework (Table 2). For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document, consult reviews (e.g., Drug-Induced Liver Injury team consultation), CDRH assessment of the AT assay, and the product quality review.
L45: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L46: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with two or more adequate and well-controlled clinical investigations. These included Study EFC14768 in subjects with HAwI and HBwI and Study EFC14769 in subjects with HA and HB as well as the comparison of subjects dosed with the AT-DR in Study LTE15174 with those in the on-demand control arms of Study EFC14768 and EFC14769.
L47: Study EFC14768 was a randomized, multicenter, open-label clinical study in 57 adult and adolescent males (aged \ge 12 years) with hemophilia A or B with inhibitors who previously received on-demand treatment with bypassing agents (BPAs) for bleeding. Eligible patients were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive fitusiran prophylaxis 80 mg SC QM (N=38) or BPA on demand for treatment of breakthrough bleeding episodes (N=19) for 9 months. In the fitusiran prophylaxis arm (80 mg QM), there was a 91% reduction in ABR for treated bleeds compared to the BPA on-demand arm (p value&lt;0.0001) in the efficacy period, demonstrating that fitusiran prophylaxis at 80 mg QM provided a substantial reduction in bleeding compared to on-demand treatment with BPAs in subjects with HAwI and HBwI.
L48: Study EFC14769 was a randomized, multicenter, open-label clinical study in 120 adult and adolescent males (aged \ge 12 years) with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors who previously received on-demand treatment with clotting factor concentrates (CFCs) for bleeding. Eligible patients were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive fitusiran prophylaxis 80 mg SC once monthly (N=80) or CFCs on demand to treat bleeding episodes (N=40) for 9 months. In the fitusiran prophylaxis arm (80 mg QM), there was a 90% reduction in ABR for treated bleeds compared to the factor on-demand arm (p value&lt;0.0001) in the efficacy period, demonstrating that fitusiran prophylaxis at 80 mg QM provided a substantial reduction in bleeding compared to on-demand treatment with CFCs in subjects with HA and HB without inhibitors.
L49: Although fitusiran was originally developed as an 80 mg QM fixed dose regimen, the dose regimen was subsequently modified to an AT-DR after observing multiple thrombotic events and determining that the risk of thrombosis was increased with persistent AT levels &lt;15%. The efficacy of fitusiran AT-DR treatment was assessed by comparing the fitusiran AT-DR treatment data from the long-term extension Study LTE15174 to the control data from studies EFC14768 and EFC14769. Of the 227 enrolled subjects in the long-term extension study, 78 subjects had inhibitors (HAWI=59 and HBWI=19) and 149 subjects did not have inhibitors (HA=115 and HB=34). In subjects with inhibitors treated with fitusiran, there was a 73% reduction (p-value&lt;0.0006) in the estimated ABR for treated bleeds in the primary efficacy period compared to those treated with BPA on demand from the control data. In subjects without inhibitors, there was a 71% reduction (p-value&lt;0.0001) in the estimated ABR for treated bleeds in subjects treated with fitusiran in the primary efficacy period compared to those treated with CFCs on-demand from the control data. In summary, the AT-DR provides substantial reduction in bleeding compared to on-demand treatment, consistent with the efficacy data from the 80 mg QM dose regimen.
L50: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L51: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L52: **Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework**
L53: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FVIII gene, which results in severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1 in 5,000 live male births. Patients are at increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life-threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and muscle bleeds, hemarthrosis, and excessive bleeding with procedures or trauma. Bleeding can occur in vital organs (including the brain). Adults commonly experience hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which is associated with significant disease morbidity.
Hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the FIX gene, which results in severe deficiency or absence of the clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1 in 30,000 live male births. Deficient factor IX levels result in an increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life-threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA.
One of the most serious complications of HA and HB is development of inhibitors to factors, which is associated with the use of factor replacement. Approximately 30-35% of patients with HA and 5-15% of patients with HB develop inhibitors. Patients with inhibitors often experience increased disease morbidity and mortality because the bleeding is more difficult to prevent or treat.
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with or without FVIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with or without FIX inhibitors are serious chronic diseases with significant morbidity and mortality due to bleeding. Patients with inhibitors tend to have a worse disease course.
L54: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	The goal of treatment in hemophilia is to prevent bleeding, improve joint function, relieve pain, and to assist the patient in continuing/resuming normal activities of daily living.
Current treatment options for patients with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors include either episodic IV infusion of the missing factor (FVIII or FIX) in response to symptoms of a bleed ("on-demand") or scheduled prophylactic factor (FVIII or FIX) administration to prevent bleeds from occurring.
Prophylactic treatment in patients with severe hemophilia with replacement factor VIII or IX, has been proven to reduce the frequency of bleeding events and joint destruction; however, it requires lifelong IV infusion every 1 to 2 weeks, resulting in treatment burden, incomplete adherence, and complications associated with indwelling catheters (e.g., infection, thrombosis). These products can also lead to inhibitor formation:Neutralizing alloantibodies (inhibitors) develop with exposure to exogenous factor and block the activity of factor VIII or IX. Approximately 30% of patients with HA and 1-15% of patients with HB will develop inhibitors against infused FVIII or FIX.
Immune tolerance induction (ITI) is used to eradicate the inhibitor, but this is only successful in 60-80% of cases in patients with HAwl and 15-30% of patients with HBwl.

Available non-factor-replacement therapies for hemophilia A or B include:Emicizumab (HEMLIBRA) is a bispecific factor-IXa- and factor-X-directed antibody that was FDA-approved in 2017 for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A with or without inhibitors. Emicizumab is administered weekly up to every 4 weeks by SC injection. Emicizumab has a boxed warning for thrombotic microangiopathy and thromboembolism associated with concomitant use of activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC).
Marstacimab (HYMPAVZI) is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) antagonist that was FDA-approved in 2024 for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA and HB without inhibitors. Marstacimab is a weekly SC injection. Marstacimab has a risk of hypersensitivity reactions, in addition to thromboembolic events.

The effectiveness of available therapy for prophylactic treatment of bleeds varies. There is a need for additional safe and effective therapies to prevent or reduce bleeding in patients with HA and HB with and without inhibitors.
Treatment with factor replacement can result in significant treatment burden, including need for IV catheters, risks of infections and thrombosis, and development of inhibitors. There is also need for available therapies that can be dosed less frequently and by a more convenient route of administration (such as SC).
L55: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	Concizumab (ALHEMO) is a TFPI antagonist that was FDA-approved in 2024 for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HAwI and HBwI. Concizumab is a daily SC injection. Concizumab has a risk of hypersensitivity reactions, in addition to thromboembolic events.
Gene therapy options are FDA-approved for patients with HA and HB; however, not all patients are candidates.
FDA-approved bypassing agents are used to prevent or control bleeding in patients with inhibitors. aPCC is indicated to control and prevent bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Recombinant activated coagulation factor VII (rFVIIa) is indicated for the treatment of bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Efficacy of these products can be variable, and they carry a boxed warning for thrombosis.
Fitusiran AT-DR routine prophylaxis compellingly reduces the frequency of bleeding episodes in subjects with HA or HB with or without inhibitors when compared with on-demand BPA (in those with inhibitors) or on-demand CFCs (in those without inhibitors).
The prevention and/or reduction of bleeding is a clinical benefit, expected to reduce the morbidity and mortality associated with hemophilia, including joint damage and hemorrhagic stroke.
Fitusiran offers the additional convenience of less frequent dosing (every 1 to 2 months) by subcutaneous route.

Studies EFC14768 and EFC14769 were multicenter, open-label clinical trials, in which patients with HA or HB with or without inhibitors were randomized 2:1 to fitusiran 80 mg QM or on-demand therapy. Both studies showed a statistically significant reduction in ABR for treated bleeds with fitusiran compared to on-demand therapy.
Study LTE15174 was a long-term extension study in which 227 subjects with HA or HB with or without inhibitors, who previously received fitusiran in EFC14768 or EFC14769, rolled over and received fitusiran AT-DR. Efficacy of fitusiran AT-DR was established by comparison of data from patients in the extension study LTE15174 with data from patients in the control arms of the parent studies.
In subjects with inhibitors treated with fitusiran prophylaxis, there was a substantial reduction in mean ABR for treated bleeds: 5.1 with 95% CI (2.8, 9.5) for AT-DR compared to 19.1 with 95% CI (11.8, 31.0) for on-demand BPA, with a resultant 73% reduction in estimated ABR for the fitusiran AT-DR compared to on-demand BPA (p=0.0006).
In subjects without inhibitors treated with fitusiran prophylaxis, there was a substantial reduction in mean ABR for treated bleeds: 9.0 with 95% CI (5.6, 14.5) for AT-DR compared to 31.4 with 95% CI (20.5, 48.2) for on-demand BPA, with a resultant 71% reduction in estimated ABR for the fitusiran AT-DR compared to CFC on-demand (p<0.0001).
L56: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	Similar reductions in the magnitude of bleeding were reported in the estimated annualized spontaneous bleeding rate (AsBR) and estimated annualized joint bleeding rate (AjBR) in the fitusiran AT-DR group for both the inhibitor and noninhibitor groups.In the inhibitor group, the mean AsBR for treated bleeds in the AT-DR group was 3.1 with 95% CI (1.8, 5.4) compared to 17.1 with 95% CI (9.9, 29.6) for on-demand BPA, with an estimated ABR reduction of 82% (p<0.0001). The mean AjBR for treated bleeds was 4.0 with 95% CI (2.5, 6.2) in the fitusiran AT-DR group compared to 14.4 with 95% CI (9.0, 23.1) for on-demand BPA, with a 73% reduction in estimated ABR with fitusiran AT-DR (p=0.0001).
In the noninhibitor group, the AsBR for treated bleeds in the AT-DR group was 5.4 with 95% CI (3.7, 8.0) compared to 21.0 with 95% CI (14.0, 31.6) for on-demand BPA, with an estimated ABR reduction of 74% (p<0.0001). The AjBR for treated bleeds was 6.2 with 95% CI (4.2, 9.2) in the fitusiran AT-DR group compared to 21.6 with 95% CI (14.6, 31.9) for on-demand BPA, with a 71% reduction in the estimated ABR with fitusiran AT-DR (p<0.0001).

Fitusiran was originally developed as an 80 mg QM dose; however, due to serious events of thrombosis associated with persistent AT activity <15%, the dosing regimen was modified to an AT-DR, targeting AT activity 15-35%.
Fitusiran's safety was evaluated primarily in 286 subjects who received fitusiran on the AT-DR.There were no fatal adverse events on the AT-DR.
Serious adverse reactions occurred in 1.4% of subjects; the most common serious adverse reaction was cholecystitis (N=2).
Adverse reactions leading to permanent discontinuation of fitusiran occurred in 1.4% of subjects and included liver injury, postoperative deep vein thrombosis, cerebral infarction, and pruritus.
Adverse reactions leading to dosage interruptions of fitusiran occurred in 0.7% patients and included increased serum transaminases.

The safety data submitted were sufficient to characterize the toxicity profile of fitusiran.
The major risks of fitusiran include thrombotic events, gallbladder events, and hepatotoxicity, all of which appear to be reduced with the AT-DR compared to fixed-dosing. It is essential that patients maintain AT activity 15-35%, necessitating the contemporaneous approval of a companion diagnostic for measuring AT activity.
The risk of thromboembolism with the use of fitusiran will be included in a Boxed Warning, together with mitigation strategies (e.g., noting use of the fixed dosing regimen is not approved, need to target AT activity 15-35%,
L57: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
The most common adverse reactions (\ge 10\%) were viral infection (29%), nasopharyngitis (26%), and bacterial infection (11%). Other reported adverse reactions (\ge 5\%) included hepatic injury (8%), arthralgia (8%), prothrombin fragment 1.2 increased (7%), injection site reaction (6%), headache (5%), and cough (5%).
There are several risks that should be included in the Warnings and Precautions section and Boxed Warning of the United States Prescribing Information (USPI). These risks were identified from the pooled safety population, which includes exposure to fitusiran at fixed doses and the AT-DR (n=355).Thromboembolic events were an adverse event of special interest based on the mechanism of action of fitusiran and the thrombotic events observed in the fitusiran fixed dosing regimen. Thrombotic events were reported in 7 subjects (2.6%) receiving fitusiran 80 mg QM prophylaxis, including a fatal cerebral venous sinus thrombosis event, and in 4 subjects (1.4%) receiving the AT-DR regimen. The risk of thrombosis was greater in patients with persistent AT activity <15\%, when bleed management guidelines were not followed in the post-operative setting, with an indwelling venous catheter, and with use of the 80 mg once monthly dosing (not the approved dosing). Monitoring of AT activity using an FDA-cleared test (approved contemporaneously as a companion diagnostic) and targeting AT in the range of 15-35% is recommended to reduce the risk of thrombosis.
A risk of acute and recurrent gallbladder disease was identified. In the fitusiran development program, 17% of subjects receiving the fixed dose once monthly regimen experienced gallbladder events, 11 of whom (24%) underwent cholecystectomy. Gallbladder events were reported in 3.8% of subjects receiving the AT-DR, one of whom underwent cholecystectomy. All but one of the patients who underwent cholecystectomy resumed treatment after surgery. One subject who started on fixed dosing experienced cholangitis and pancreatitis caused by gallstone disease more than a year after cholecystectomy while receiving AT-DR dosing.
Hepatotoxicity was identified as a risk with the use of fitusiran. On the 80 mg once a month fixed dose regimen, alanine transaminase (ALT) and aspartate transaminase (AST) elevations above 3 times

and need to follow bleeding management guidelines.
Fitusiran has a unique and unexpected risk of acute and recurrent gallbladder disease, which resulted in the need for surgical intervention in some subjects. Even routine surgeries in patients with hemophilia can be challenging due to bleeding complications. It is critical that prescribers are aware of this risk before prescribing fitusiran in order to identify signs/symptoms early and to consider whether fitusiran is the appropriate option for their patient. Therefore, this risk will be included in a Boxed Warning, together with mitigation strategies (e.g., considering alternative treatment for hemophilia in patients with a history of symptomatic gallbladder disease; considering interruption or discontinuation of fitusiran if gallbladder disease occurs).
The risk of hepatotoxicity will be described in a Warning and Precaution, together with mitigation strategies (e.g., monitoring liver tests, potential need to interrupt or discontinue fitusiran).
We will also require enhanced pharmacovigilance for thrombotic events, hepatotoxicity, and gallbladder disease (including cholelithiasis and cholecystitis) to further monitor these risks in the postmarketing setting.

L61: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L62: Hemophilia is a rare, serious, and chronic hematologic disorder caused by an inherited deficiency in factor VIII (hemophilia A) or factor IX (hemophilia B), leading to an increased risk of minor and life-threatening bleeding. Therapy is either given on demand, in which the replacement factor or BPA is given at the time of bleeding, or as prophylaxis, in which the therapy is continuously administered to prevent bleeding from occurring. Treatment with clotting factor concentrate can lead to inhibitor development, a serious complication in which bleeding is more difficult to manage. Newer treatments are available for patients depending on the hemophilia subtype and presence or absence of inhibitors, including emicizumab, marstacimab, and concizumab. Despite available therapies, new treatment options are needed.
L63: The efficacy data for fitusiran prophylaxis demonstrate a large, compelling reduction in ABR using AT-DR compared to on-demand treatment with BPA (in those with hemophilia A or B with inhibitors) and compared to CFC (in those with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors). The prevention of bleeding, a clinical benefit, is expected to reduce the morbidity and mortality associated with hemophilia. Fitusiran has the added benefit of every 1- to 2-month SC dosing, based on the AT level; a less frequent administration of the drug is beneficial for patients who may take it lifelong.
L64: The risks of fitusiran can adequately be described with labeling. Thrombotic events and acute and recurrent gallbladder disease will be included in a Boxed Warning and the risk of hepatotoxicity will be included in the Warnings and Precautions. The incidence of these three risks was lower with the AT-DR compared to the non-approved fixed-dosing regimen; therefore, monitoring of AT activity and targeting AT activity in the range of 15-35% is essential for the safe and effective use of fitusiran. An FDA-cleared AT activity assay is being contemporaneously approved with fitusiran as a companion diagnostic. These risks are further mitigated with instructions in labeling (e.g., need to follow bleed management guidelines to reduce thrombotic risks; need to consider alternative hemophilia therapy in patients with a history of symptomatic gallbladder disease; need to monitor liver tests while on treatment) and will be communicated to patients in a Medication Guide.
L65: The large benefit of fitusiran on reducing bleeding outweighs the identified risks when the mitigation approaches described in labeling are followed, providing another useful treatment option for patients with this serious, life-threatening disease. Labeling recommends use of fitusiran by healthcare providers experienced in the treatment of hemophilia, who are the most likely prescribers. These prescribers are well-versed in managing these complex diseases and determining the best treatment option for their specific patients considering the benefits and risks of the available therapies.
L66: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L67: ## 3. Introduction
L68: Fitusiran (proprietary name QFITLIA), a new molecular entity, is a double-stranded small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA) that causes degradation of antithrombin (AT) messenger RNA (mRNA) through RNA interference, reducing plasma AT levels. AT is a serine protease inhibitor that inactivates thrombin (factor IIa) and factor Xa, along with other enzymes in the coagulation system. Lower plasma AT protein levels lead to increases in thrombin generation and an improved ability to form stable clots. Fitusiran acts independently from factor VIII (FVIII) and factor IX (FIX), and thus would not be expected to result in the formation of factor-neutralizing antibodies.
L69: The Applicant proposes the following indication: for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adults and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with hemophilia A (HA) (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with or without factor VIII inhibitors, or hemophilia B (HB) (congenital factor IX deficiency) with or without factor IX inhibitors.
L70: The proposed dosing regimen is 50 mg subcutaneously once every 2 months, with dose adjustments to maintain AT activity between 15 and 35% using an FDA-authorized AT activity assay.
L71: ## Disease Background
L72: Congenital hemophilia is a rare X-linked bleeding disorder caused by deficiencies of FVIII in HA and FIX in HB. Patients with HA or HB have a deficiency in the prothrombinase complex, essential for the conversion of prothrombin to thrombin, which is crucial for hemostasis (Sidonio et al. 2023). HA and HB occur in all racial and ethnic groups and are due to missense, nonsense, or deletions in *F8* or *F9*, respectively. The prevalence of HA is estimated at 24.6 cases per 100,000 male births, while HB prevalence is estimated at 5 cases per 100,000 male births; in both HA and HB, approximately 30 to 40% of cases are sporadic, or born to a noncarrier mother (Seaman et al. 2021; Sidonio and Malec 2021). The severity of hemophilia is correlated with residual factor levels, with mild defined as between 5 and less than 40% FVIII or FIX activity, moderate as between 1 to less than 5% activity, and severe as less than 1% activity (Srivastava et al. 2020). Severe disease occurs in roughly 30% of HB and 40% of HA. Inhibitors to FIX occur in 5 to 15% of severe HB, whereas inhibitors to FVIII occur in 30% of severe HA. Differences in severity and inhibitor development between HA and HB are related to the differences in the size of the respective genes, and the types of genetic lesions that differ between HA and HB (Castaman and Matino 2019).
L73: Clinical hallmarks of severe hemophilia are spontaneous or traumatic joint, soft tissue, and muscle hemorrhages (Sidonio et al. 2023), and prolonged and potentially fatal hemorrhage after trauma or surgery. In patients with HA or HB, neutralizing antibodies known as FVIII or FIX inhibitors, respectively, may develop, rendering exogenous factor replacement ineffective. The main risk factors for inhibitor development are hemophilia severity, *F8/F9* genotype, and
L74: cumulative exposure to FVIII and FIX concentrates (Rezende et al. 2024). Individuals with hemophilia with inhibitors are more difficult to treat in response to bleeding events, leading to a higher rate of progressive joint disease from frequent joint bleeds and increased risk of death from uncontrolled bleeding.
L75: The goal of treatment in severe hemophilia is to prevent or minimize the occurrence of bleeding to improve joint function, alleviate pain, and help the patient to continue/resume regular daily activities. Treatment with replacement factor can either be in response to a bleeding event (“on-demand”) or scheduled to prevent bleeds by maintaining factor levels in a protective range (prophylactic treatment). There is significant evidence that prophylactic treatment in children with severe hemophilia prevents bleeding episodes and the associated joint damage (Manco-Johnson et al. 2007). In individuals with severe hemophilia, an early start of treatment with long-term prophylaxis is reported to reduce bleeding frequency and subsequent chronic joint disease and result in better quality of life, and is recommended by international hemophilia care organizations (Srivastava et al. 2020; Rezende et al. 2024). The choice of regimens (i.e., on-demand or prophylaxis) is multifactorial and may be based on frequency and severity of bleeding, patient preference and lifestyle, and regional/geographic standard of care. The World Federation of Hemophilia strongly recommends prophylactic treatment for patients with severe hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors to prevent bleeds at all times. Prophylaxis should be individualized (adjusting prophylaxis dose/frequency or both), taking into consideration patient bleeding phenotype, joint status, individual pharmacokinetics, and patient self-assessment and preference (Konkle et al. 2019).
L76: The treatment options for severe hemophilia have advanced from plasma-derived factor replacement to recombinant factor replacement, extended half-life recombinant factor replacement, non-factor-replacement therapy, and gene therapy. Annualized bleeding rate (ABR) has become the primary outcome measure to demonstrate the success of treatment products for severe hemophilia (Konkle et al. 2019). ABR is a clinically meaningful endpoint, as a reduction in bleeding events is important to patients by decreasing risk of mortality and organ and tissue damage, including joint damage, and allowing greater activity that might otherwise be limited due to risk of trauma and bleeding.
L77: ## **Current Therapeutic Options**
L78: The current prophylaxis treatment options for patients with severe HA or HB without inhibitors include:
L79: - Prophylactic treatment with replacement factors, which has been proven to reduce the frequency of bleeding events; however, this approach requires frequent IV infusion, resulting in high treatment burden, incomplete adherence, complications associated with indwelling catheters (e.g., infection, thrombosis) and can lead to the development of factor inhibitors. Replacement factor therapy includes both plasma-derived factor concentrates and recombinant factor concentrates.
- Non-factor-replacement therapies have been designed to provide an alternative to conventional replacement treatment and either increase thrombin generation through the inhibition of naturally occurring anticoagulants, or by mimicking the action of factor VIII. These agents are designed to work in the prophylaxis context, are not indicated for
L80: management of acute bleeding episodes, and are typically dosed subcutaneously on either weekly or monthly schedules.
L81: - Emicizumab (Hemlibra) subcutaneous (SC) injection is a bispecific FIXa- and factor X (FX)-directed antibody, approved for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with HA with and without inhibitors. The emicizumab product label (Genentech 2024) contains a boxed warning that states cases of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events were reported when, on average, a cumulative amount of &gt;100 U/kg/24 hours of activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) was administered for 24 hours or more to patients receiving emicizumab prophylaxis.
- Marstacimab (Hym pavzi) is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) antagonist indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA or HB without inhibitors. Dosing is weekly SC. Thromboembolic events may occur and are listed in warnings and precautions (Pfizer Laboratories Div Pfizer Inc 2025).
3. Adeno-associated virus (AAV)-based gene therapy introduces an AAV vector that inserts a functional copy of the missing clotting factor gene into patients' hepatocytes so that clotting factor can be expressed (Deshpande et al. 2024). Advantages of gene therapy include a one-time infusion and factor expression levels that provide excellent prophylaxis. Although these therapies appear to have few serious adverse events, a notable toxicity of hepatotropic AAV-based gene therapy is liver toxicity, which can result in loss of transgene expression of factor.
L82: The current prophylaxis treatment options for patients with severe HA or HB without inhibitors is detailed in Table 3.

L88: The current prophylaxis treatment options for patients with severe hemophilia A with inhibitors (HAWI) or hemophilia B with inhibitors (HBWI) include:
L89: - Non-factor-replacement therapiesEmicizumab (Hemlibra) is also approved for HAWI; see description of the product above.
- Concizumab (Alhemo) is a TFPI antagonist indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients 12 years of age and older with HA or HB with inhibitors. Alhemo is dosed daily via SC injection. Thromboembolic events may occur and are listed as a warning in the label (Novo Nordisk 2024).
- Bypassing agents, including recombinant activated factor VII or activated prothrombin complex concentrates, are limited by short half-lives requiring daily IV infusion and by cost. Bypassing agents can be used for prophylaxis in both HA and HB with inhibitors. Bypassing agents are also associated with thrombotic risk.

L102: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L103: Table 5 provides an overview of the clinical studies conducted to support the benefit-risk assessment of fitusiran for subjects with HA and HB with and without inhibitors.
L104: Results from subjects with HA and HB with and without inhibitors from the parent studies EFC14768 and EFC14769, and the extension study LTE15174 formed the primary basis for the assessment of efficacy. The primary assessment of efficacy for the fitusiran 80 mg QM dosage was based on the parent studies, EFC14768 and EFC14769. The assessment of efficacy for the AT-DR was based on a comparison of efficacy data from subjects who received the AT-DR during the single arm extension study LTE15174 to those who used on demand treatment during the parent studies EFC14768 and EFC14769.
L105: The assessment of risk for the fitusiran AT-DR was primarily based on pooled analyses of subjects who received the AT-DR from Studies LTE15174, LTE14762, and EFC15110 (N=286). Additionally, assessment of risk was based on data from a pooled safety set comprising all adverse events occurring in subjects with HA or HB with or without inhibitors while receiving any dosage of fitusiran in a clinical study (N=335).

L133: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L134: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L135: Hemophilia A and hemophilia B are inherited X-linked genetic bleeding disorders characterized by a deficiency in blood clotting factors in the intrinsic coagulation pathway, which causes an increased risk of bleeding in affected patients. Antithrombin (AT) is a liver-expressed anticoagulant protein that is the major endogenous inhibitor of the coagulation factor thrombin. Fitusiran (AD-57213 or ALN-AT3SC) is a synthetic double-stranded small interfering RNA (siRNA), designed to suppress liver production of AT as a strategy to rebalance the coagulation cascade by increasing thrombin generation to improve hemostasis in individuals with hemophilia. Fitusiran contains a conjugated triantennary N-acetylgalactosamine (GalNAc) sugar ligand that binds to the asialoglycoprotein receptor (ASPGR) on hepatocytes (Cummings and
L136: McEver 2009). Upon binding, the ASPGR-fitusiran complex is internalized and processed in endosomes, where fitusiran is loaded onto the RNA-induced silencing complex (RISC), which guides fitusiran to the target AT mRNA sequence (transcribed from the *SERPINC1* gene) and utilizes RNA interference mechanisms to specifically target/degrade AT mRNA. Reduced AT mRNA levels result in lowered plasma AT protein levels, increased thrombin generation potential, and improved ability to form stable clots (Figure 1).
L137: **Figure 1. Mechanism of Action of Fitusiran**
L138: Source: Applicant's Application Orientation Meeting
Coagulation model depicting two separate initiations, intrinsic (contact) and extrinsic pathways, which ultimately merge at the level of factor Xa (common pathway). In hemophilia A and B, the lack of FVIII and FIX, respectively, leads to a decrease in thrombin potential, resulting in a bleeding phenotype.
Abbreviations: F, factor
L139: ## 5.1.1. In Vitro Characterization of Fitusiran
L140: To establish the proof of concept for fitusiran as a potential treatment for hemophilia, a series of in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies was conducted by the Applicant. The ability of fitusiran to reduce AT mRNA, protein, and activity levels was characterized in vitro using HepG2 cells (immortalized human liver cells) and evaluated in vivo using various wild-type (WT) species, transgenic mouse models of hemophilia A (HA) and B (HB), an HA dog model (genetic), and an HA monkey model (anti-FVIII antibody). The specificity of fitusiran for the AT mRNA was evaluated using bioinformatics methodology and in vitro in HepG2 cells.
L141: ## 5.1.2. In Vitro Pharmacological Activity of Fitusiran
L142: Addition of fitusiran to HepG2 cells in vitro resulted in a dose-dependent inhibition of AT mRNA levels with a half maximal inhibitory concentration of 49.6pM. There was no appreciable off-target inhibition detected for any of the eight potential off-target transcripts identified by bioinformatics analyses and tested in HepG2 cells at up to 10nM fitusiran, which supported the specificity of fitusiran for the AT mRNA. Refer to Section 13 for additional details on these studies.
L143: ## 5.1.3. In Vivo Proof of Concept Animal Model Data
L144: Fitusiran is pharmacologically active in mice, rats, dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys, as the AT mRNA target sequence is fully conserved (mice, rats, monkeys) or highly conserved (dogs) with the human sequence. AT levels were assessed following single and repeated subcutaneous administrations of fitusiran in WT mice and healthy monkeys, and in mouse, monkey, and dog models of hemophilia. In WT mice, single SC doses of fitusiran &gt;10 mg/kg led to dose-dependent AT suppression exceeding 80%. Consistent with the long tissue residence typical of siRNA drugs, AT mRNA suppression was maintained for at least 28 days postdosing. A single SC dose of fitusiran up to 2 mg/kg in healthy monkeys resulted in AT protein reductions of up to 70%. Further AT reductions up to 80% were observed following 6 months of repeated monthly dosing. Consistent with the intended therapeutic mechanism, reduction of AT was associated with increased thrombin generation. These data confirmed the ability of fitusiran to hit the intended target in vivo and suggested that a less frequent dosing regimen might be suitable for the intended hemophilia patient population.
L145: Mouse models of hemophilia have been generated by disruption of the genes encoding clotting factor VIII (modeling hemophilia A) or factor IX (modeling hemophilia B). These models exhibit bleeding tendencies similar to those observed in humans with hemophilia (Sabatino et al. 2012). A single SC administration of fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg in hemophilia A (factor VIII knockout) mice resulted in a dose-dependent suppression of AT expression of &gt;80\% at \ge 10 mg/kg when compared to untreated HA mice. Similarly, a single-dose SC administration of fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg fitusiran in hemophilia B (factor IX knockout) mice resulted in a dose-dependent suppression of AT of up to 90% compared to untreated HB mice. A dose-related increase in endogenous thrombin up to 155% was observed at 30 mg/kg in HB mice when compared to control HB mice, predicting an improved ability of the hemophilia mice to form clots. Fitusiran activity was sustained for the duration of a 26-week repeat-dose study in HA mice; once-weekly administration of fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg/week resulted in a significantly decreased activated partial thromboplastin time of up to 60% and up to 90% suppression of plasma AT protein levels compared to control HA mice. Studies in mouse hemophilia models provided important proof of concept data and supported the initiation of clinical development of fitusiran for the treatment of hemophilia. The HA and HB mouse proof of concept data were further corroborated by studies in a genetic dog model of HA and in an HA monkey model where hemophilia was induced using an anti-FVIII monoclonal antibody (MAb). Details of these studies can be found in Section 13.
L146: Functional improvements in hemostatic endpoints in HA (factor VIII knockout) mice were assessed using the microvessel laser injury method to compare the ability to form clots in fitusiran-treated versus untreated animals. HA mice were given a single SC administration of fitusiran up to 30 mg/kg prior to injury. Ten days later, the cremaster muscle was injured by laser to induce bleeding. A dose-related increase in the accumulation of platelets and fibrin and improved thrombus formation within 3 minutes were observed in fitusiran-treated HA mice, while no clotting was detected in PBS-treated HA mice. Stable thrombus formation was similarly demonstrated in HB (factor IX knockout) mice using the same methodology. These studies in clinically relevant mouse models link the fitusiran mechanism of action data (decreased AT mRNA, protein expression, and activity) with the desired clinical outcome of reduced clotting times in the presence of injury and suggest that fitusiran can be used prophylactically in the clinic (as opposed to use as a rescue treatment).
L147: ## NDA 219019
## QFITLIA (fitusiran)
L148: Overall, fitusiran administration in healthy animals and hemophilia animal models resulted in dose-dependent decreases in AT mRNA, protein, and activity; decreased activated partial thromboplastin time; and increased endogenous thrombin formation, which supported the proposed mechanism of action. Fitusiran caused sustained reductions of AT mRNA that were anticipated based on the long tissue residence times observed with other siRNA drugs in animals and humans; these data supported the development of less frequent clinical dosing regimens (e.g., once every 8 weeks). Administration of fitusiran prior to laser injury in hemophilia mice led to consistent clot formation at the injury site, demonstrating the desired functional improvement in clinically relevant hemophilia models with real world implications; these data support a plausible mechanism of action by which fitusiran may prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes. Overall, the results from the studies support the therapeutic rationale for the use of fitusiran as a potentially effective treatment for patients with hemophilia A or B.
L149: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology / Pharmacokinetics

L151: Characteristic	Drug Information
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Fitusiran is an antithrombin-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid
Mechanism of action	Fitusiran is a double-stranded small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA) that causes degradation of antithrombin (AT) messenger RNA through RNA interference, which results in a reduction of plasma AT levels.
Active moieties	Fitusiran siRNA solution for SC administration
QT prolongation	A thorough QT (TQT) study was not conducted in humans with fitusiran because data from nonclinical and clinical studies provide the totality of evidence that fitusiran is not associated with QTc prolongation up to 80 mg QM.
Bioanalysis	Plasma fitusiran concentration was quantified using a validated liquid chromatography with fluorescence detection (LC-FD) method
Healthy subjects versus patients	Based on the population PK analysis, hemophilia type and inhibitor status do not impact the PK of fitusiran.
Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen (or single dose, if more relevant for the drug)	The plasma PK parameters of fitusiran following 20 and 50 mg are shown below:

Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	No maximum tolerated dose was identified. The highest dose studied was 80 mg QM.
Dose proportionality	Fitusiran exhibited a dose-proportional increase in plasma exposure over the dose range of 20 to 50 mg following single-dose SC administration.
Accumulation	No accumulation of fitusiran was observed following multiple once weekly (QW) or QM doses, as fitusiran is short-lived in plasma.
Time to achieve steady state	From a pharmacodynamic standpoint, regardless of the dose regimen, >99% of subjects are expected to reach steady state AT activity levels within 23 weeks following initiation of fitusiran dosing or following dose escalation or de-escalation.

L154: Characteristic	Drug Information
Body weight	Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Based on PopPK analysis, body weight and age were identified as statistically significant covariates in subjects with hemophilia, but the magnitude of the effect (<25% on fitusiran PK) is not considered to be clinically relevant (fitusiran effect is driven by fitusiran concentrations in liver rather than plasma).
Age	See above
Renal impairment	No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal impairment. The PopPK analysis using clinical trial data from subjects with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) >45 mL/min/1.73 m2 identified eGFR as a statistically significant covariate in subjects with hemophilia, but the magnitude of the effect (<25% on fitusiran plasma PK) is not considered to be clinically relevant (fitusiran effect is driven by fitusiran concentrations in liver, rather than plasma).
Hepatic impairment	No dedicated study has been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic impairment. There was no clinical experience of fitusiran in subjects with hepatic impairment, as the clinical development program excluded subjects with clinically significant liver disease, or with ALT and/or AST >1.5× upper limit of normal reference range (ULN) or total bilirubin > ULN. Given the risk for hepatotoxicity, the review team recommends “avoid use” in patients with all degrees of hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A, B and C).
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
At clinically relevant concentrations, fitusiran is unlikely to be an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5, or to be an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	At clinically relevant concentrations, fitusiran is unlikely to be an inhibitor of major efflux transporters, including P-gp, BCRP, and BSEP, or major uptake transporters.
Bioanalysis	Immunogenicity (if Applicable)
Antidrug antibodies (ADAs) against fitusiran were assayed using ELISA. No assay for neutralizing antibody (NAb) was developed.
Incidence	During fitusiran treatment (up to 250 weeks) in four pivotal studies, 10 out of 290 adults with hemophilia (3.4%) developed ADAs. All subjects had low ADA titers and the majority of subjects had transient ADAs.
Clinical impact	There was no identified clinically significant effect of ADAs on pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, or effectiveness of fitusiran.

L157: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L158: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L159: ## 6.1.1. Applicant's Proposed Dosing Regimen
L160: The proposed starting dose of fitusiran is 50 mg once subcutaneously every 2 months (Q2M) and then, if needed, the dose can be escalated or de-escalated to maintain the AT activity in the range of 15 to 35%. The doses include 50 mg QM, 20 mg Q2M, or 20 mg QM based on AT activity levels. AT activity must be measured using an FDA-cleared AT activity assay (under review by the Center for Devices and Radiological Health as a Companion Diagnostic). No dose adjustment from 50 mg Q2M is needed if AT activity levels are maintained between 15 to 35%. AT activity should be measured prior to initiation of fitusiran
L161: After a dose modification, AT measurements should be restarted. The Agency made recommendations to add a 10 mg Q2M dose (described in Section 6.1.4.1) and also to update the frequency and timing of AT activity measurement (described in Section 6.1.4.2) in Section 2 of the USPI.
L162: ## 6.1.2. Selection of Initial Dosing Regimen for Parent Phase 3 Studies
L163: ## Selection of the Initial 80 mg QM Dose Regimen
L164: The Applicant's selection of the initial 80 mg QM dosing regimen for the phase 3 study was based on the results of phase 1 study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001) as well as modeling and simulation. Due to an observed disconnect between fitusiran plasma pharmacokinetic (PK) and AT activity, predicted fitusiran concentrations in the liver RISC (based on allometrically scaled total liver PK parameters from rat liver PK) and observed clinical PD values (AT activity) were utilized for the development of a population kinetic-pharmacodynamic (PopK-PD) model. The Applicant's PopK-PD modeling and simulation analyses appeared reasonable for guiding dosing regimen development. Refer to Section 14 Pharmacometrics Assessment for detailed evaluation of this model.
L165: The phase 1 study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001) evaluated once-weekly (QW) and QM regimens, both as body-weight-based (mg/kg) doses and as fixed (mg) doses. The tolerability and safety of fitusiran in the phase 1 study were evaluated in 3 healthy subjects after a single SC administration of 0.03 mg/kg, and in 42 subjects with hemophilia A or B after either three QW doses ranging from 0.015 to 0.075 mg/kg, three QM doses ranging from 0.225 to 1.8 mg/kg, or three QM doses of 50 or 80 mg. A dose-dependent reduction in AT activity from baseline was observed at all dose levels (Figure 2). Among tested doses, 50-mg and 80-mg fitusiran doses demonstrated generally similar safety profiles and reduction in AT activity.
L166: In addition to the observed dose-AT-activity relationship, the Applicant conducted an exploratory PopK-PD analysis to evaluate reduction in AT activity over a fitusiran dose range of 1 to 140 mg. The results showed that with doses greater than 80 mg fitusiran, only a small incremental change in AT activity was predicted (Figure 3).

L171: Based on evaluation of the mean annualized bleeding rate (ABR) by AT activity quartile observed in the phase 1 study TDR14767 (ALN-AT3SC-001), it was determined that an AT activity reduction of &gt;75% showed maximal effect in controlling bleeding, as shown in Figure 4 below.

L175: PopK-PD simulations to predict AT reduction at steady state indicated that 50 mg QM dosing would result in 87% of subjects achieving &gt;75% AT reduction, while 80 mg QM dosing would result in 98% of subjects achieving &gt;75% AT reduction (POH1133).
L176: Based on the totality of available observed and simulated data, the Applicant chose the initial 80 mg QM fitusiran dose for the phase 3 studies (EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110), with the goal of achieving maximal pharmacodynamic (PD) activity (AT reduction) with low intersubject variability.
L177: ## 6.1.3. Development of AT-DR
L178: The fitusiran phase 3 program was initiated at a fixed dose regimen of 80 mg QM, and later adjusted to an AT-based dosing regimen (AT-DR).
L179: The efficacy and mean AT levels with fitusiran 80 mg QM treatment compared with on-demand treatment with BPAs or CFCs, or compared with prior BPA/CFC prophylaxis are summarized in Table 8 below.

L183: Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; AT, antithrombin; BPA, bypassing agent; CFC, clotting factor concentrate; HA/B, hemophilia A or B; SD, standard deviation
L184: One potential concern with the 80 mg QM dose is that the mean AT levels are close to 10% with fitusiran treatment. Clinical data suggested that the risk of serious thrombotic events may be greater with persistent AT levels &lt;10%.
L185: In October 2020, when the phase 1/2 study (LTE14762), the three parent phase 3 studies (EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110), and the phase 3 long-term extension (LTE) study (LTE15174) were ongoing, the Applicant placed a voluntary pause on ongoing clinical trials to evaluate the risk of the 80 mg QM regimen, due to concerns regarding thrombotic events potentially related to fitusiran.
L186: After analyzing the role of AT reduction and associated risk of thrombotic events as well as bleeding episodes, the Applicant revised the dosing regimen to AT-DR with a target AT activity of 15 to 35%. The 15% AT activity was set as the lower threshold because clinical data as of October 2020 suggested that the risk of serious thrombotic events may be greater with AT levels &lt;10%, while AT activity from 15% to 25% was deemed to preserve efficacy. The AT activity 35% was set as the upper threshold because data as of October 2020 suggested that AT levels &lt;25% (&gt;75% AT reduction) showed maximal effect in controlling bleeding, and the upper bounds of the 95% and 90% confidence intervals (CIs) of effective AT levels (25%) were 35.5% and 34.2%, respectively.
L187: With the identification of AT target thresholds (15 to 35% of normal AT activity), the Applicant performed modeling and simulation to identify the doses to achieve this target range. Based on the simulation results, the starting dose was revised to 50 mg Q2M (instead of 80 mg QM), with an option to sequentially escalate to 50 mg QM and 80 mg QM in case of AT activity &gt;35% and options to decrease the dose or discontinue fitusiran for AT levels less than 15%. This revised AT-based dosing regimen was implemented in December 2020 across studies (except EFC14768 and EFC14769, which were nearly complete at the time of the voluntary dosing pause). After implementation of the AT-DR, the observed number of subjects who had to discontinue fitusiran treatment due to AT activity levels &lt;15% exceeded predictions. Therefore, based on additional simulations, the lower dose of 20 mg Q2M was identified, with the option to escalate to 20 mg QM in case of AT activity &gt;35%. This lower dose regimen was subsequently implemented in the
L188: long-term extension part of Study LTE15174. The final AT-DR employed in Study LTE15174 is depicted in Figure 5 below.
L189: **Figure 5. Final Revised AT-Based Fitusiran Dose Regimens (LTE15174)**
L190: Source: Module 5.3.5.2 Study LTE15174 Appendix 16.1.1 [Protocol].
L191: Abbreviations: AT, antithrombin; LTE, long-term extension; Q2M, every 2 months; QM, once monthly; SS, steady state
L192: During the parent phase 3 studies and LTE15174, the Applicant found that escalation to 80 mg QM did not seem necessary, because none of the subjects who started dosing at 50 mg Q2M required dose escalation beyond 50 mg QM up to 80 mg QM. Therefore, in the final proposed AT-DR, the Applicant kept the AT-DR implemented since December 2020, but removed the option of escalating to 80 mg QM.
L193: ## 6.1.4. Evaluation of AT-DR
L194: ## AT-DR Is Supported by Positive Efficacy and Safety Results
L195: The efficacy and safety evaluation supporting the AT-DR is discussed in detail in Section 6.2 and Sections 7.4 to 7.6.
L196: ## AT-DR Is Effective in Maintaining AT Activity in the Target 15 to 35% Range
L197: As shown in Figure 6 below, in Study LTE15174, the target AT range of 15 to 35% could be achieved with the AT-DR, and 94% of subjects required 0 or 1 dose adjustment to achieve the target AT activity range.

L201: AT-DR with the lowest dose option of 20 mg Q2M resulted in additional subjects reaching the target AT range at steady state, but a lower dose option is needed to keep more subjects on treatment.
L202: **Table 9** shows the observed and simulated subject distribution across the evaluated dose regimens. Both the observed and simulated subject distribution showed that 50 mg Q2M resulted in the largest percentage of subjects reaching the target AT activity range 15 to 35% at steady state. Thus, a starting dose of 50 mg Q2M was determined to be appropriate because it would allow the highest percentage of subjects to start fitusiran treatment at the dose which would enable them to achieve AT activity target sooner than other options.

L209: ## 6.1.4.1. Recommendation for Adding 10 mg Q2M for Patients Whose AT Level Drops Below 15%
L210: The review team simulated AT activity at Week 76 (close to trough) for two regimens: 10 mg Q2M and 15 mg Q2M. Table 10 shows that approximately 6% (out of the 7% discontinued due to AT &lt;15%) of virtual subjects could remain on treatment with the addition of a 10 mg Q2M regimen. The 10 mg Q2M regimen is expected to result in relatively more patients remaining on treatment than 15 mg Q2M for patients with at least one AT activity &lt;15% following 20 mg Q2M. The lower dose option of 10 mg Q2M will be an added dosage option in product labeling.

L215: ## 6.1.4.2. Changes to Proposed AT Monitoring Plan for Dose De-Escalation
L216: The review team revised the Applicant's proposed AT monitoring and dose adjustment instructions (see Section 14.5.4 Evaluation of AT monitoring program). Specifically, changes were made to measure AT activity at Weeks 4 (Month 1), 12 (Month 3), and 20 (Month 5) that coincide with the lowest (nadir) AT activity (4 weeks post-dose) following Q2M or QM dosing to aid in dose de-escalation decisions. A measurement at Week 24 (Month 6) was recommended to capture the pre-dose AT activity to aid in dose escalation decisions. The revised instructions are shown below:
L217: Measure AT activity prior to initiation of QFITLIA using an FDA-authorized test. Do not initiate QFITLIA dosing if AT activity is &lt;60% at baseline, (b) (4)
L218: Measure AT activity at Weeks 4 (Month 1), 12 (Month 3), 20 (Month 5) and 24 (Month 6) following the starting dose and after any dose modification.
L219: - If any AT activity is &lt;15%, a dose reduction is required, the lower dose should be initiated 3 months after the prior dose.
- If AT activity is &gt;35% after 6 months, or if the subject has not achieved satisfactory bleed control, dose escalation should be considered.
L220: Once the subject's target dose is identified based on AT activity 15 to 35%, (b) (4) measure AT activity annually. Additional measurements can be considered (b) (4)
L221: ## 6.1.4.3. Dosing in Adolescents
L222: The use of fitusiran in adolescents with hemophilia A and B is supported by the three randomized phase 3 studies (EFC14768, EFC14769, EFC15110) and one open-label long-term extension study (LTE15174). A total of 60 adolescents were evaluated in the clinical studies. The dosing used in adolescents was the same as used in adults. No differences in efficacy were observed between the adolescent subgroup and the overall population (see Sections 6.2.1.5 and 6.2.2.5, efficacy subgroup analysis). In general, the adverse reactions in fitusiran-treated adolescent subjects were similar in type to those seen in adult subjects with hemophilia A or B, with or without inhibitors (see Section 7.5.2.9 safety subgroup analysis).
L223: The PopPK analysis also showed that age (12 to 71 years) did not have a significant effect on the PK of fitusiran (Table 11).

L230: ## 6.1.4.4. Bridging of Phase 3 to the To-Be-Marketed Product Presentations
L231: The vial/syringe formulation was used in phase 1 to 3 studies, but a prefilled pen (PFP) was introduced during LTE15174 for administration of 50-mg doses in recognition that some subjects might prefer its convenience. The Applicant proposes both vial/syringe and PFP formulations in the label (each PFP is designed to deliver a fixed dose of 50 mg of fitusiran in 0.5 mL, and each vial is designed to deliver 20 mg of fitusiran in 0.2 mL). Patients on 50 mg having to decrease the dose to 20 mg should switch from the PFP to vial/syringe presentation. A scientific bridge between PFP and vial/syringe presentations was established from a PK and efficacy perspective.
L232: From a PK perspective, comparison of fitusiran PK administered via the PFP and vial/syringe was performed as part of phase 3 study LTE15174. In Study LTE15174, 12 subjects were first administered the 50-mg fitusiran dose using the glass vial with syringe, and then the next dose of 50 mg was administered with PFP. Eleven subjects completed both glass vial with syringe and PFP dose administrations. As shown in Table 12 below, the two formulations were bioequivalent with the 90% CIs of maximum plasma concentration, area under the concentration-time curve to
L233: the last measurable concentration (AUC_{last}) and area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity (AUC_{\infty}) falling within the bioequivalence acceptance range of 80 to 125%.

L241: Overall, the proposed AT-DR, with the addition of the 10-mg lower dose option and revised AT monitoring plan, is reasonable from a clinical pharmacology perspective.
L242: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L243: In support of the proposed indication, the Applicant initially conducted three phase 3 parent studies (i.e., EFC14768 [ATLAS-INH], EFC14769 [ATLAS-A/B], and EFC15110 [ATLAS-PPX]) using a fixed fitusiran dose of 80 mg monthly to evaluate the efficacy of fitusiran in adults and adolescents with hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors. Study EFC14768 was a randomized, open-label, controlled trial in subjects with hemophilia A or B with inhibitors. Study EFC14769 was a randomized, open-label, controlled trial in subjects with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors. Study EFC15110 was a single-arm trial in subjects with hemophilia A or B with or without inhibitors who were switched to fitusiran from factor or BPA prophylaxis.
L244: In October 2020, the Applicant received reports of nonfatal vascular thrombotic events in subjects on fitusiran in the ongoing phase 3 program. Pending further assessment of these events, and in accordance with the recommendations of an external, independent Data Monitoring Committee and Scientific Steering Committee, the Applicant decided to voluntarily pause dosing and enrollment in all ongoing phase 3 clinical studies using the 80 mg QM regimen as of October 30, 2020, and notified the Agency on November 2, 2020. The original statistical analysis
L245: plan for summary of clinical efficacy/integrated summary of effectiveness was submitted on December 24, 2020.
L246: The Applicant proposed a revised dose regimen and restarted the evaluation of the drug in a long-term extension study (LTE15174 [ATLAS-OLE]). The fitusiran treatment period of the two parent phase 3 studies EFC14768 and EFC24769, was completed as of January 26, 2021. Following completion of these parent studies, subjects rolled over into the long-term extension study. Subjects who have not completed study EFC15110 will also roll into the long-term extension study after study completion. The revised dose (AT-DR) in the long-term extension study consists of a starting dose of 50 mg Q2M, with options to escalate to 50 mg QM and 80 mg QM, and to de-escalate to 20 mg Q2M and 20 mg QM. The dose administered is determined on an individual basis in order to maintain AT level between 15 to 35%.
L247: When this new dosing regimen was implemented, Studies EFC14768 and EFC14769 were near completion; thus, the AT-DR was not implemented in either of these studies. In switching to Study EFC15110, the 50 mg Q2M dose was implemented in 2 subjects. The AT-DR was fully implemented in the extension Study LTE15174. Due to the change in dosing regimen, efficacy of fitusiran AT-DR was established by a comparison of data from subjects in the extension study LTE15174 with the data from subjects in the control arms of the parent studies.
L248: ## **Pool A (HA or HB With Inhibitor) and On Demand Therapy: Intersubject Comparison**
L249: ## **Treatment Arm**
L250: AT-DR fitusiran treatment data were generated from the extension Study LTE15174. Only AT-DR fitusiran treatment data from subjects in Study LTE15174 who were randomized to the fitusiran arm in Study EFC14768 (with inhibitor population) were used in the primary analysis (see Figure 7).
L251: ## **Control Data**
L252: BPA control data were taken from the original phase 3 Study EFC14768. Control data from subjects who were randomized to the BPA arm in Study EFC14768 were used in the primary analysis (see Figure 7).
L253: ## **Pool B (HA or HB Without Inhibitor) and On Demand Therapy: Intersubject Comparison**
L254: ## **Treatment Arm**
L255: AT-DR fitusiran treatment data were generated from the extension Study LTE15174. Only AT-DR fitusiran treatment data from subjects who were randomized to the fitusiran arm in Study EFC14769 (without inhibitor population) were used in the primary analysis (see Figure 7).
L256: ## **Control Data**
L257: Factor on demand control data were taken from the original phase 3 Study EFC14769. Control data from subjects who were randomized to the factor on demand arm in Study EFC14769 were used in the primary analysis (see Figure 7).
